FILE: README.md
================================================
# ML Agent Workspace

## Build Instructions

To build the agent workspace container on your local machine, run the following command in the terminal:

```Bash
docker compose build agent-workspace
```

This will build the container that you will use for development on your Mac.

```Bash
docker compose build cpu-runner
```

When you are ready to execute your training script on the GPU server, you will need to build the CUDA image. Run the following command on the server:

```Bash
docker compose build gpu-runner
```

## Execution Instructions

### On Mac (Development):

You will boot the interactive terminal exactly as you have been doing to let the agent code:

```Bash
docker compose run --rm agent-workspace bash
```

### On the GPU Server (Execution):

Once you have pushed your main branch to GitHub and pulled it down to the server, you will run the GPU container to execute the W&B training script:

```Bash
docker compose run --rm gpu-runner python src/process_all.py
```

```Bash
# This builds the CUDA image and runs your training script
docker compose run --rm gpu-runner python src/train.py --epochs 50 --batch_size 128
```

```Bash
# This maps the GPU, mounts the repo, and runs exactly one month of data
docker compose run --rm gpu-runner python src/train.py \
  --epochs 2 \
  --batch_size 128 \
  --start_date "2023-03-02" \
  --end_date "2023-04-02"
```

## Hyperparameter Tuning (Optuna)

The tuning entry point is `src/tune.py`. It launches an Optuna study and logs trial metrics to Weights & Biases through `WeightsAndBiasesCallback`.

The search space is defined in `configs/sweep.yaml`:

- `lr` (log-uniform)
- `w_forecast` (uniform)
- `w_impute` (uniform)

### Local CPU Smoke Sweep (Mock Data)

Use this first to validate the full tuning loop quickly on your Mac:

```Bash
docker compose run --rm cpu-runner python src/tune.py --use_mock_data
```

If you want to keep runs local-only and avoid syncing to W&B cloud:

```Bash
docker compose run --rm cpu-runner bash -lc 'WANDB_MODE=offline python src/tune.py --use_mock_data'
```

### GPU Server Sweep (Real Data)

Run this on the GPU server after pulling the latest code and setting your `.env`:

```Bash
docker compose run --rm gpu-runner python src/tune.py
```

Recommended explicit run (select your exact data window and stream):

```Bash
docker compose run --rm gpu-runner python src/tune.py \
  --n_trials 2 \
  --epochs 2 \
  --batch_size 4 \
  --start_date 2023-10-01 \
  --end_date 2023-10-04 \
  --veh_class person \
  --dir all
```

### Logs and Outputs

- Sweep logs are written to `logs/optuna_sweep_YYYYMMDD_HHMMSS.log`
- Best parameters and best validation loss are printed at the end of the run

### Tune CLI Options

`src/tune.py` now supports CLI overrides so you do not need to edit source code for most sweep changes:

- `--n_trials`
- `--epochs`
- `--batch_size`
- `--start_date`
- `--end_date`
- `--seq_len`
- `--veh_class`
- `--dir`
- `--use_mock_data`

Show all options:

```Bash
docker compose run --rm cpu-runner python src/tune.py --help
```

### Troubleshooting: Empty Data Window / Filter Errors

If your selected `--start_date`, `--end_date`, `--veh_class`, or `--dir` produces no rows, tuning will fail fast with a clear error explaining which filter removed all rows and which values were available in the loaded data. Adjust the date window or stream arguments and rerun.

## W&B Local Storage Management

When using Weights & Biases Artifacts for model storage, W&B will download models to a local cache (usually `~/.cache/wandb/artifacts/`) when they are requested by inference or export scripts. To ensure your local environment doesn't run out of space over time, you can periodically run the following command to delete older cached models (this will *not* delete them from the cloud):

```Bash
# Keep only the last 5GB of downloaded artifacts locally
wandb artifact cache cleanup 5GB
```



================================================
FILE: docker-compose.yml
================================================
services:
  # Run locally on mac for development.
  agent-workspace:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile.agent
    container_name: my-ml-agent
    volumes:
      - .:/workspace
    # Notice we don't even need the 'environment:' block anymore!
    # Compose passes the .env file automatically.
    stdin_open: true
    tty: true

  # Isolated Frontend Environment
  frontend-dev:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile.frontend
    container_name: nextjs-frontend
    volumes:
      - ./frontend:/workspace/frontend
      # ANONYMOUS VOLUME: Prevents node_modules from syncing back to the host
      - /workspace/frontend/node_modules
      - /workspace/frontend/.next
    ports:
      - "3000:3000"
    working_dir: /workspace/frontend
    # Command can be overridden here, but usually baked into Dockerfile.frontend
    command: npm run dev

  # Run on GPU server for training.
  gpu-runner:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile.gpu
    container_name: gpu-executor
    volumes:
      - .:/workspace
    # We explicitly pass the WANDB_API_KEY from the server's .env file
    environment:
      - WANDB_API_KEY=${WANDB_API_KEY}
      - GOOGLE_PLACES_API_KEY=${GOOGLE_PLACES_API_KEY}
    # This block maps the physical server GPUs into the container
    deploy:
      resources:
        reservations:
          devices:
            - driver: nvidia
              count: all
              capabilities: [gpu]

  # Run on local CPU server for testing.
  cpu-runner:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile.cpu
    container_name: cpu-executor
    volumes:
      - .:/workspace
    # We explicitly pass the WANDB_API_KEY from the server's .env file
    environment:
      - WANDB_API_KEY=${WANDB_API_KEY}
      - GOOGLE_PLACES_API_KEY=${GOOGLE_PLACES_API_KEY}


================================================
FILE: Dockerfile.agent
================================================
# Use a lightweight Python base image
FROM python:3.11-slim

# Prevent interactive prompts during apt installations
ENV DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive

# 1. Install system dependencies (Adding curl and gnupg for Node.js)
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y \
    curl \
    git \
    build-essential \
    unzip \
    gnupg \
    && apt-get clean && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*

# 2. Install Node.js (Required for Gemini)
RUN curl -fsSL https://deb.nodesource.com/setup_20.x | bash - \
    && apt-get install -y nodejs \
    && apt-get clean && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*

# 3. Install the Agents
# Install Gemini via npm
RUN npm install -g @google/gemini-cli
# Install Claude via native binary
RUN curl -fsSL https://claude.ai/install.sh | bash \
    && CLAUDE_BIN="$(find /root/.local/share/claude/versions -maxdepth 1 -type f | sort | tail -n 1)" \
    && test -n "$CLAUDE_BIN" \
    && ln -sf "$CLAUDE_BIN" /usr/local/bin/claude \
    && chmod +x /usr/local/bin/claude

# 4. Set up the working directory inside the container
WORKDIR /workspace

# 5. Copy your ML requirements and install them
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# Set the default command to open a bash shell
CMD ["/bin/bash"]

FILE: Dockerfile.cpu
================================================
FROM python:3.11-slim

WORKDIR /workspace

# Install system dependencies
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y \
    build-essential \
    git \
    && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*

# Install Python dependencies
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# The code will be mounted at runtime
CMD ["/bin/bash"]



================================================
FILE: Dockerfile.frontend
================================================
# Dockerfile.frontend
#
# Isolated Node.js environment for the Phase 1 Static Prototype frontend.
# Aligned with NSTAR_MLOPS.md §1 ("Strict Container Isolation & Anonymous Volumes")
# and NSTAR_FRONTEND_ARCHITECTURE.md §1 (Core Tech Stack).
#
# Container Isolation Contract ("Zero Host Download"):
#   - All npm packages are baked into the image at build time via `npm ci`.
#   - At runtime, docker-compose mounts /workspace/frontend/node_modules and
#     /workspace/frontend/.next as anonymous volumes; this preserves the
#     in-image installs while the rest of /workspace/frontend is overlaid by
#     the host bind mount (enabling cross-container hot-reload from the
#     agent-workspace container into frontend-dev).
#   - This image is strictly decoupled from the PyTorch backend
#     (Dockerfile.cpu / Dockerfile.gpu): no Python, no CUDA, no torch.
#
# Tech stack pinned by frontend/package.json:
#   Next.js 16.2.4, React 19.2.4, TypeScript (strict),
#   Deck.gl 9.3.1, MapLibre GL 5.23.0, Zustand 5.0.12.

FROM node:20-bookworm-slim

# Development image: Next.js dev server with HMR. Telemetry disabled to
# keep the container hermetic and the build logs clean.
ENV NODE_ENV=development \
    NEXT_TELEMETRY_DISABLED=1

WORKDIR /workspace/frontend

# 1. Copy manifest + lockfile FIRST so the dependency layer is cached
#    independently of source-code churn.
COPY frontend/package.json frontend/package-lock.json ./

# 2. Reproducible install. `npm ci` honours the lockfile exactly, refuses
#    to mutate it, and writes only into /workspace/frontend/node_modules
#    (which becomes the seed of the runtime anonymous volume).
RUN npm ci

# 3. Copy the rest of the frontend source tree. At runtime the host bind
#    mount overlays /workspace/frontend with the developer's working tree;
#    this COPY ensures the image is independently buildable and runnable
#    (e.g. CI smoke tests, or any environment where the bind mount is absent).
COPY frontend/ ./

# 4. Expose the Next.js dev server port (matched by the docker-compose
#    `frontend-dev` service port mapping 3000:3000).
EXPOSE 3000

# 5. Launch the Next.js dev server bound to 0.0.0.0 so the Mac host
#    browser can reach the container via http://localhost:3000.
#    Default localhost binding inside the container is unreachable
#    from the host network namespace.
CMD ["npm", "run", "dev", "--", "-H", "0.0.0.0", "-p", "3000"]



================================================
FILE: Dockerfile.gpu
================================================
FROM pytorch/pytorch:2.1.0-cuda12.1-cudnn8-runtime

WORKDIR /workspace

# Install system dependencies
RUN apt-get update --fix-missing && apt-get install -y \
    build-essential \
    git \
    && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*

# Install Python dependencies
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# The code will be mounted at runtime
CMD ["/bin/bash"]

FILE: eda_summary.md
================================================
# Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA) Summary

## 1. Datasets Overview

### Traffic Data (`traffic_20230108_20260108.parquet`)
- **Size:** 21,144,475 records, 6 columns.
- **Columns:** `category` (str), `dir` (str), `dt` (datetime64), `location` (str), `value` (int64), `veh_class` (str).
- **Date Range:** 2023-03-02 to 2026-01-08.
- **Description:** Contains traffic measurements (e.g., flow) at various sensor locations broken down by vehicle class (e.g., 'person', 'car') and direction.

### Weather Data (`weather_20230108_20260108.parquet`)
- **Size:** 2,715,140 records, 13 columns.
- **Columns:** `sensor_name` (str), `variable` (str - e.g., 'Humidity', 'Solar Radiation'), `timestamp` (datetime64), `value` (float64), `units` (object), `flagged` (bool), `location_wkt` (str), `sensor_centroid_latitude` (float64), `sensor_centroid_longitude` (float64), `sensor_height_above_ground` (float64), `ground_height_above_sea_level` (float64), `broker_name` (str), `raw_id` (str).
- **Date Range:** 2023-01-08 to 2025-10-20.

### Sensor Metadata JSONs
- **`traffic_sensors.json`:** Maps string `location` identifiers to numeric `lat` and `lon` coordinates.
- **`weather_sensors.json`:** Maps `Sensor_Name` identifiers to `Location_WKT`, `Sensor_Centroid_Latitude`, `Sensor_Centroid_Longitude`, and elevation details.

---

## 2. Likely Target Variable
The likely target variable for predictive modeling is **`value`** in the **Traffic dataset**. 
Since `Congestion_Level` is not present, `value` corresponds to the quantitative traffic flow/volume or speed metric at a given location and time. The goal of an ML pipeline here would likely be to predict the future traffic `value` using historical traffic patterns and correlated weather phenomena.

---

## 3. Data Quality Summary

### Missing Values
- **Traffic Data:** While the data dictionary may suggest a clean dataset with **0 missing values**, this is because missing data in this table does not appear as explicit `NaN` values. Instead, the time series is **irregular and contains missing timestamps (gaps)**.
    - **Temporal Gaps:** The standard measurement frequency should be **15 minutes**. However, the time series is highly irregular: **~23% of gaps** between consecutive sensor readings are larger than 15 minutes, meaning countless timestamps have simply been dropped from the dataset rather than being recorded as null.
    - **Zero-Count Deletion:** Only 1,964 records (out of 21.1M) have a `value` of 0. This confirms that missing periods and zero-traffic moments were omitted. This irregular structure must be corrected via temporal resampling/re-indexing before sequential modeling.
- **Weather Data:** The **`units`** column is entirely empty (2,715,140 missing values) and should be dropped. All other 12 columns contain **0 missing values**.

### Outliers and Anomalies
- **Negative Traffic Values:** The `value` column in the traffic dataset has a minimum value of **-27**. Assuming this represents a physical count of vehicles or traffic flow, negative numbers are physically impossible and represent anomalous readings, sensor errors, or special null-equivalent codes (e.g., similar to a -99 placeholder). These must be filtered or imputed before training.
- **Traffic Maximums:** The maximum `value` is 2,691, with a mean of 13.3 and a 75th percentile of 10. The large max value may represent sudden heavy bursts of traffic or aggregated anomalies.

### Class Imbalances / Distributions
- **Traffic `category`:** Heavily imbalanced towards `'flow'` (20.5M out of 21.1M records). 
- **Traffic `veh_class`:** The most frequent class is `'person'` (6.8M records), though there are 16 unique vehicle classes overall.
- **Traffic `dir` (Direction):** 'east_to_west' is the most heavily represented direction (4.1M records) out of 122 unique directions.
- **Weather `variable`:** Dominated by `'Humidity'` (1.38M out of 2.7M records) and collected predominantly by the `aq_mesh_api` broker.

## 4. Next Steps for Pipeline Design
1. **Data Cleaning:** Drop `units` from weather data. Filter or handle negative `value` anomalies in the traffic dataset.
2. **Temporal Alignment:** Re-index the traffic dataset to a continuous 15-minute time series (resampling and filling gaps with zeros or imputed values) to restore physical continuity.
3. **Data Integration:** Use spatial coordinates (from the JSON files) to map traffic sensors to the nearest weather sensors, subsequently merging the dataframes on datetime (`dt` and `timestamp`) and location identifiers.
4. **Feature Engineering:** Extract temporal features (hour, day of week, month) from the datetimes, pivot weather variables to columns, and aggregate traffic volumes to a consistent time-frequency (e.g., hourly).

| 2026-04-16 | main              | Fix mathematical flaw in val_zero_acc batch aggregation.                                                                                                                                                                           | Identified that averaging per-batch zero_acc percentages introduces sample-count bias (batches with fewer zeros skew the epoch metric). Refactored calculate_metrics in src/metrics.py to return zero_true_count (int) and zero_correct_count (int) instead of a float percentage; removed the fallback 1.0 for no-zero batches. Refactored src/train.py validation loop to accumulate raw integer counts across all batches and compute val_zero_acc = zero_correct_total / zero_true_total post-loop (0.0 when no zeros exist in the epoch). Added tests/test_metrics.py (9 unit tests covering all count branches, type assertions, and absence of legacy zero_acc key). Also fixed pre-existing test_pipeline.py unpack arity mismatch. All 13 pytest tests pass. (1) Feature Scaling: Added sklearn StandardScaler to RealIoTDataset.**init** in src/dataset.py — dynamic_data [T, N, 3] flattened to 2D, fit_transform'd, reshaped back; static_features [N, 2] likewise scaled. Both scalers saved as self.scaler / self.static_scaler for future inverse transforms. (2) Decoder Deepening: Replaced single nn.Linear layers in both impute_layer and forecast_layer (src/decoders.py) with 2-layer MLPs: Linear(hidden_dim, hidden_dim*2) → GELU → Dropout(0.1) → Linear(hidden_dim*2, out_dim). Decoder param count increased from ~390 to 17,414. (3) tune.py refactored to use a make_objective factory with --use_mock_data CLI flag for local integration testing. Local 2-trial Optuna sweep (WANDB_MODE=offline, mock data) completed without errors; best trial val_loss=5.21. Updated test_decoders.py gradient assertion to index into nn.Sequential. | 2026-04-16 | main              | Fix ZINB domain contradiction from StandardScaler + add analysis hooks.                                                                                                                                                            | Root cause: StandardScaler converted non-negative traffic counts to signed floats, making target*forecast always non-integer and rarely == 0, which crashed the ZINB NLL and suppressed zero_true_total to 0. (1) Dataset fix: saved self.unscaled_traffic = val_arr.copy() before stacking with weather features; **getitem** now pulls target_forecast from self.unscaled_traffic[idx+seq_len] with .clamp(min=0) guard. Dynamic encoder input still uses scaled self.dynamic_data. (2) Encoder analysis hook: MaskedSpatiotemporalEncoder.forward() accepts return_attention_weights=False; when True, GATv2Conv is called with return_attention_weights=True and (x_spatial, (*, alpha)) is unpacked; returns (x_out, alpha). (3) Pipeline analysis mode: ResilientIoTPipeline.forward() accepts return_analysis=False; when True, encoder is called with return_attention_weights=True and returns {'edge_index', 'edge_weight', 'attention_weights'} without computing loss. (4) train.py logging upgraded: ZeroAcc line now shows [zero_correct/zero_true] raw counts; val_zero_true_count and val_zero_correct_count added to W&B. Verification: 10-epoch run on real Oct 2023 data (53 nodes, 3 days, 23.5% zero-count targets) completed without NaN. zero_true_total=660 per epoch confirms targets are now correct non-negative integers. ZeroAcc=0/660=0.00 is mathematically correct — pi stabilises at ~0.46 because optimal pi for 23.5% structural-zero data is ~0.23, well below the 0.5 threshold; ZeroAcc>0 requires more epochs. All 13 pytest tests pass. | 2026-04-29 | main              | Finalise container-isolation strategy: create `Dockerfile.frontend` for the Phase 1 frontend.                                                                                                                                      | Authored `Dockerfile.frontend` (`node:20-bookworm-slim`, `WORKDIR /workspace/frontend`, `NODE_ENV=development`, `NEXT_TELEMETRY_DISABLED=1`). Layered build: (1) copy `frontend/package.json` + `frontend/package-lock.json`, (2) `npm ci` to bake all dependencies into the image (Next.js 16.2.4, React 19.2.4, Deck.gl 9.3.1, MapLibre GL 5.23.0, Zustand 5.0.12, Tailwind 4, Vitest 4.1.5 — full lockfile honoured, no host writes), (3) copy the remaining `frontend/` source as a build-time fallback, (4) `EXPOSE 3000`, (5) `CMD ["npm","run","dev","--","-H","0.0.0.0","-p","3000"]` so the Mac host browser can reach the dev server. Compliance: NSTAR_MLOPS.md §1 "Zero Host Download" — packages live exclusively in the image's `node_modules` layer, which docker-compose's anonymous volumes for `/workspace/frontend/node_modules` and `/workspace/frontend/.next` will preserve at runtime against the `./:/workspace` bind mount overlay; NSTAR_FRONTEND_ARCHITECTURE.md §1 tech stack pinned by `frontend/package.json`; NSTAR_PHASES.md "Decoupled Parallelism" — image contains no Python/CUDA/torch and is strictly decoupled from `Dockerfile.cpu` / `Dockerfile.gpu`. Layer-cache ordering (manifest+lockfile before source COPY) keeps incremental rebuilds fast under typical TS/TSX edits. Note: docker-compose.yml does not yet declare a `frontend-dev` service or its anonymous-volume mappings — wiring the service is a follow-up task and is intentionally out of scope here per CLAUDE.md §7 "Piecewise Execution". Outcome: `Dockerfile.frontend` written and ready to be referenced by a `frontend-dev` service in `docker-compose.yml` (next step). (1) `src/pipeline.py` `return_analysis=True` branch now also returns `imputed_features` ([B, N, T, D]); previously dropped via `*, mu, theta, pi = self.decoders(...)`. (2) `src/export_ui_payload.py`: enabled `mask_prob=0.15`(was 0.0 — needed to populate non-trivial mask*series); captured`imputed_features`and`node_mask`; added length-96 per-node arrays `ground_truth_series`(raw counts pulled from`dataset.unscaled_traffic[idx+seq_len-1]`, clamped ≥0; `null`for the 5 simulated nodes),`imputed_series`(last-step traffic channel inverse-transformed via`x_raw = scaled \* dataset.scaler.scale*[0] + dataset.scaler.mean\*[0]`— scale=36.5223, mean=23.5619), and`mask_series`(boolean from`node_mask[:,-1,0]`; all-true for simulated nodes). Tripwires: assert `imputed_features.shape == (1, 50, 12, 3)`, assert `scaler.scale\*[0] > 0`, assert no NaNs in `imputed_features`, plus per-node post-write assertions on array length and is_simulated invariants. **DESK CHECK** (CLAUDE.md §8) executed and PASSED for window_idx=84, real node id=2 (mask=true at last step): expected `imputed_series[0] = 5.9378`from raw scaled tensor → JSON value`5.9378`(Δ=0.000000); ground_truth`unscaled_traffic[95, 2] = 3.0`matches JSON`3.0`. Mask density measured: 648/4320 = exactly 15.00%. Output: `data/processed/ui_payload.json`5.01 MB, 50 nodes (45 real + 5 simulated), 96 timesteps, 2500 edges. **Frontend.** (1)`types/payload.ts`extended`NodeEntry`with`ground_truth_series: (number\|null)[]`, `imputed_series: number[]`, `mask_series: boolean[]`. (2) `store/useTimeStore.ts`added`selectedNodeId: number\|null = null`, `attentionThreshold: number = 0.05`, with setters; `setAttentionThreshold`clamps to [0,1]. (3)`MapVisualizer.tsx`split into two ScatterplotLayers:`nodes-real` (`stroked:false, filled:true`) and `nodes-simulated` (`stroked:true, filled:false, lineWidthMinPixels:3`) — Deck.gl flags are layer-wide so single-layer was insufficient. Added `onClick`(sets selectedNodeId) on both node layers; map background click clears selection. ArcLayer rewritten: filters by`attention_sequence[t] >= attentionThreshold`; if `selectedNodeId != null`, ego-graph isolation keeps only edges where source==selected or target==selected; otherwise defensive `slice(0, GLOBAL_EDGE_CAP=80)`. `useEffect`deps +`updateTriggers`extended with`selectedNodeId`and`attentionThreshold`. (4) New `NodeInspector.tsx`(right-side floating panel,`position:absolute right:16 top:16 w:360`): renders only when `selectedNodeId != null`; SVG chart (no Recharts dep) overlays ground truth (blue polyline, splits on null) vs imputed (orange polyline) over a length-96 horizontal axis; rose-tinted vertical bands behind masked timesteps; dashed cursor at `currentTimestep`; close button; SIMULATED badge; current-step readout (gt, imputed, µ, σ); embedded threshold slider for live ArcLayer filtering. (5)`MapScene.tsx`mounts`NodeInspector`via`dynamic({ssr:false})`(Next.js 16 App Router rule). **Verification.**`tsc --noEmit`→ 0 errors. Tabs scaffold gains Tab 4 "ZINB Calibration". Zero frontend math — all aggregated arrays served directly from payload. |

FILE: NSTAR_BACKEND_ARCHITECTURE.md

# NSTAR_BACKEND_ARCHITECTURE.md

## Objective

Implement an end-to-end, joint spatiotemporal graph neural network in PyTorch and PyTorch Geometric (PyG). The model is designed for an urban IoT sensor network predicting a configurable target traffic stream (defined by vehicle class and spatial direction) every 15 minutes. It must be self-healing, capable of dynamic graph inference, missing feature imputation, and uncertainty-aware forecasting. **The pipeline must remain strictly univariate per run** to prevent gradient interference across different vehicle classes.

## Core Paradigm

The system abandons the I.I.D. assumption, leveraging Graph Attention Networks (GATs) to pass messages along dynamically learned topological connections, alongside Transformers for temporal sequence modelling.

### 1. Architectural Components

The pipeline (`ResilientIoTPipeline`) consists of three primary modules sharing a latent space:

#### Dynamic Graph Learner (Topology Inference):

- Takes static node features (e.g., coordinates, sensor types).
- Uses a multi-head GAT (or linear embeddings with pairwise similarity) to learn a dynamic adjacency matrix on the fly.
- Applies a ReLU and a sparsity threshold, converting the dense matrix to PyG's `edge_index` and `edge_weight` using `dense_to_sparse`.

#### Masked Spatiotemporal Encoder (Feature Extraction):

- Takes artificially masked dynamic time-series data and the inferred graph structure.
- **Spatial:** Uses PyG `GATv2Conv` layers to aggregate neighbourhood features.
- **Temporal:** Flattens the spatial output and passes it through a standard PyTorch `TransformerEncoder`.

#### Decoupled Decoders (Multi-Task Learning):

- **Imputation Decoder:** An MLP (e.g., Linear -> GELU -> Dropout -> Linear) applied across the entire sequence to reconstruct the masked historical data.
- **Uncertainty-Aware Forecasting Decoder:** An MLP taking the final time step's embedding. Outputs three parameters representing a Zero-Inflated Negative Binomial (ZINB) distribution: $\mu$ (mean), $\theta$ (dispersion), and $\pi$ (zero-inflation probability).

### 2. Data Pipeline Specifications

The PyTorch Dataset must yield a dictionary for each batch. Target variables must strictly remain as raw, unscaled counts, while input dynamic/static features must be normalized via `StandardScaler` to prevent gradient starvation.

### 3. Joint Training & Loss Logic

The model must be trained simultaneously on all tasks. Total Loss = $(w_{forecast} \times \mathcal{L}_{ZINB\_NLL}) + (w_{impute} \times \mathcal{L}_{impute\_MSE})$.

- **ZINB Tripwires:** Apply `F.softplus` to $\mu$ and $\theta$ raw logits, plus an `eps=1e-6` buffer to mathematically guarantee they never equal zero.

### 4. Model Development & Ablation Strategy

Before reaching final production architecture, the pipeline must undergo structured evaluation:

- **Baseline Benchmarking:** Before tuning GNN hyperparameters, visualize a "Last Observation Carried Forward" (LOCF) or Historical Average model. The deep learning pipeline must visually and mathematically outperform these dumb baselines.
- **Zero-Inflation ($\pi$) Tuning:** Do not rely on a hardcoded `0.5` threshold to determine structural zeros. Plot the ROC curve for the $\pi$ parameter to find the optimal threshold for traffic shut-offs (e.g., nighttime periods).
- **Component Ablation:** Systematically disable the `DynamicGraphLearner` and swap the `GATv2Conv` for temporal-only baselines to isolate the exact performance delta generated by the graph topology.

FILE: NSTAR_FRONTEND_ARCHITECTURE.md
================================================
# NSTAR_FRONTEND_ARCHITECTURE.md

## Objective

Implement a Geospatial Digital Twin for the Resilient IoT network. This frontend must visualize complex spatiotemporal graph inferences, including Zero-Inflated Negative Binomial (ZINB) predictions, mathematical uncertainty bounds, dynamic graph attention weights, and deep explainability metrics (e.g., self-healing imputation logs). It must be highly performant, capable of rendering thousands of animating nodes and edges at 60fps across a sliding time window.

## 1. Core Tech Stack

- **Framework:** Next.js (React) with TypeScript (Strict mode enabled).
- **Geospatial Engine:** **Deck.gl** (by Uber) running over a **MapLibre GL JS** base map. Standard mapping libraries (Leaflet, Mapbox standard) are banned for data layers to prevent DOM overload. Deck.gl WebGL layers must be used for all data rendering.
- **State Management:** **Zustand**. Required to decouple the rapid time-slider updates from the React render cycle, preventing the entire DOM from re-rendering when only WebGL properties need to change.
- **Database & Spatial Backend (Future Phase):** Supabase (PostgreSQL + PostGIS) for road-network mapping and spatial interpolations.
- **Live Inference API (Future Phase):** FastAPI (Python) wrapping the frozen PyTorch model.

## 2. Architectural Components (The Visual Layers)

The UI is built on independent, composable Deck.gl layers and HTML overlays that consume the backend payload.

**CRITICAL - Defensive Rendering:** The frontend must be strictly defensive. If an ablation study on the backend removes a component (e.g., removing the GAT removes attention weights), the frontend must gracefully degrade by hiding the affected layer, rather than crashing on undefined properties.

### A. The Node Layer (Predictions & Uncertainty)

- **Component**: Deck.gl ScatterplotLayer.
- **Data Source**: Sensor locations (real) and Simulated Nodes.
- **Visual Mapping**:
  - **Fill Color (Uncertainty)**: Mapped to the 95% Confidence Interval derived from the ZINB $\theta$ (dispersion) parameter.
  - **Stroke/Halo Radius (Prediction)**: Mapped to the ZINB $\mu$ parameter (mean predicted traffic). A wider radius indicates higher predicted values. Uses a logarithmic scale to accommodate the wide range of traffic volumes.
  - **Simulated Nodes**: Visually distinct (e.g., using a dashed stroke or distinct shape) to indicate the prediction is a zero-shot topological inference, not a physical sensor.

### B. The Topology Layer (Attention Weights)

- **Component**: Deck.gl `ArcLayer` or `LineLayer`.
- **Data Source**: The attention_sequence extracted from the GATv2Conv layers.
- **Visual Mapping:** Opacity/Width mapped to the attention weight ($\alpha$). Must be aggressively filtered to top _N_ edges to prevent visual clutter.

### C. The Road-Constrained Heatmap (Spatial Interpolation)

- **Component:** Deck.gl `PathLayer` rendering GeoJSON LineStrings.
- **Mechanism:** PostGIS functions (`ST_ClosestPoint`, `ST_Distance`) interpolate the ZINB $\mu$ values between adjacent sensors to color-code continuous road segments representing traffic flow.

## 3. State Management & Time Travel

- **The Global Clock:** A single integer state (`currentTimestep`) managed by `useTimeStore`.
- **Interpolation Logic:** Deck.gl layers must linearly interpolate positions, colors, and radii between `timestep[t]` and `timestep[t+1]` for smooth animation.
- **Data Slicing:** The React components do not filter the massive data payload. Deck.gl layers receive the full data arrays and use **DataFilterExtension** (a WebGL shader extension) to efficiently hide/show data on the GPU based on `currentTimestep`.

## 4. Phased Implementation Strategy

### Phase 1: The Static Prototype (Memory-Bound)

### Phase 1: The Static Prototype (Memory-Bound)

- **Goal:** Validate the visual paradigm without setting up databases or APIs.
- **Mechanism:** The Next.js app loads `ui_payload.json` directly into browser memory.
- **Deliverables:** A fully functioning, interactive Deck.gl frontend that operates completely independently of the active Python training environment.

### Phase 2: PostGIS & Live API Integration (Post-Ablation)

- **Goal:** Live inference and Road-Constrained Heatmaps.
- **Mechanism:** ONLY executed after the PyTorch architecture is mathematically frozen via rigorous ablation studies.
- **Integrational Bottlenecks to Address:**
  - **The Rolling Window Cache:** The FastAPI server cannot scan raw Parquet files for live sequences. An in-memory cache (e.g., Redis) must be used to buffer exactly 12 rolling timesteps for instant tensor generation.
  - **Tensor-to-Geospatial Alignment:** The API must maintain a strict, version-controlled hashing dictionary mapping PyTorch integer indices to physical sensor UUIDs/Coordinates to prevent visualization shifting if sensors go offline.

FILE: NSTAR_MLOPS.md
================================================
# MLOps & Experimentation Blueprint

## 1. Environment Separation (Docker)

We maintain a strict separation between development and execution using two separate Docker environments defined in `docker-compose.yml`:

- **Development (`Dockerfile.agent`):** Run locally on Mac. Contains CLI tools, Node.js, and bash dependencies for the agent to write, test, and unit-validate code on CPU subsets.
- **Execution (`Dockerfile.cpu`):** Run locally on Mac. A CPU-only image used for quick iteration and debugging of the training pipeline before server deployment.
- **Execution (`Dockerfile.gpu`):** Run on the GPU Server. A headless, optimized CUDA/PyTorch image used strictly for training and Optuna sweeps.
- **Frontend (`Dockerfile.frontend`):** Run locally on Mac. Isolated Node.js environment running the Next.js server.

**Parallel Execution Rule:** Because the GPU runner mounts the local directory (`.:/workspace`), you cannot run multiple branches from the same physical directory. To run experiments in parallel, clone the repository into separate directories on the server and execute `docker compose` independently in each.

### Strict Container Isolation & Anonymous Volumes

This project adheres to a "Zero Host Download" rule. To prevent `npm` dependencies from bleeding onto the host server's filesystem via standard bind mounts, the `frontend-dev` service utilizes an **Anonymous Volume** for `/workspace/frontend/node_modules` and `/workspace/frontend/.next`.

- All frontend packages are baked into the image during the `Dockerfile.frontend` build phase.
- If new packages must be installed dynamically during development, they must be executed _inside_ the frontend container context using `docker compose exec frontend-dev npm install <package>`. Do not run `npm install` from within the `agent-workspace` container, as it lacks the anonymous volume mapping and will corrupt the host filesystem.

### Cross-Container Hot-Reloading

The agent writes sources code (.ts, .py, etc.) inside `agent-workspace`. Because both the agent and frontend containers share the ./frontend bind mount, code changes made by the agent are instantly synced to `frontend-dev`, triggering Next.js hot-reloads. The agent must NEVER run frontend execution commands (npm run dev) inside its own workspace.

### The Sync or Sink Dependency Rule

While Python's global pip install inside the agent-workspace does not pollute the host filesystem, it creates fatal ephemeral container drift. If a new Python package is required for the PyTorch pipeline, it must be installed using docker compose exec agent-workspace pip install <package>. Crucially, any dynamically installed package MUST be immediately appended to ./requirements.txt. Failure to sync dependencies will cause the headless GPU execution environment (Dockerfile.gpu) to fail fast with a ModuleNotFoundError during automated Optuna sweeps.

## 2. File Architecture

We utilize a Centralized Configuration pattern. **No hyperparameters should be hardcoded in Python execution scripts.**

- `configs/train.yaml`: The single source of truth for all runs, containing nested dictionaries for `data`, `model`, `training`, and `loss`.
- `configs/sweep.yaml`: The W&B Optuna search space definitions.
- `src/train.py`: The orchestrator for a single training run. Responsible for model instantiation, W&B initialization, training loops, and validation logic.
- `src/train.py`: Accepts `--config configs/train.yaml`. Loads the dictionary, initializes the W&B run, and executes the pipeline.
- `src/tune.py`: Loads the base `train.yaml`, overwrites the specific parameters Optuna is currently testing, and executes `train.py`.
-

## 3. Weights & Biases (W&B) Logging Standards

- `train.py` must initialize `wandb.init(project="resilient-iot", config=config_dict)` using the full nested YAML dictionary.
- Required tracked metrics: `train_loss_total`, `train_loss_forecast`, `train_loss_impute`, `val_loss_total`, and human-readable metrics (`val_rmse`, `val_mae`, `val_zero_acc`).
- `val_zero_acc` must be aggregated by keeping a running tally of true zero counts vs. predicted zero counts across the entire epoch to avoid batch-averaging skew.

## 4. Evaluation Branching Strategy

- The evaluation framework (`train.py`, `tune.py`, metrics calculations) must be locked to the `main` branch.
- Experimental model architectures live on their own branches (e.g., `exp/pure-transformer`).
- Experimental branches will `git rebase main` to inherit updates to the universal evaluation framework.

## 5. The Static Data Bridge & Evaluation Constraints

The bridge between the PyTorch Backend and Next.js Frontend is the `export_ui_payload.py` script, which generates `ui_payload.json`.

- **The Visual Holdout Set (No Data Leakage):** The data used to generate the UI payload must be strictly quarantined from the training and validation loops. Visualizing training data in the frontend displays memorization, not predictive inference.
- **Artifact Twinning:** Every generated `ui_payload.json` must be injected with metadata identifying exactly how it was created. This includes the `wandb_run_id`, `git_commit_hash`, and a copy of the `train.yaml` hyperparameters. This ensures a 1-to-1 traceability between a visual frontend artifact and the backend model that generated it.

## 6. AI Agent Orchestration & Roles

Development in this repository is assisted by AI Agents. To maintain code quality and prevent degradation, agents are divided into three distinct roles. Do not allow agents to break character or cross responsibilities.

- **The Interrogator (@interrogator):** Read-only scout. Responsible for searching the codebase, reading files, analyzing logs, and providing context. Never writes code.
- **The Architect (@architect):** System designer. Responsible for cross-referencing requirements with `NSTAR` documents, creating step-by-step plans in `plan.md`, and performing mathematical "Desk Checks" before any code is written. Does not write execution code.
- **The Implementer (@implementer):** The executor. Writes the code based _only_ on the Architect's plan. Operates strictly within the Dockerized environment.

## 7. Environment & Dependency Isolation

This project operates on a strict **Zero Host Download** policy.

- **No Local Environments:** The creation of local virtual environments (e.g., `python -m venv`) is strictly prohibited.
- **Docker Execution:** All code execution, testing, and linting MUST take place inside the designated Docker containers using `docker compose exec [container_name] [command]`.
- **The "Sync or Sink" Rule:** Any Python dependency installed inside the container (`pip install <package>`) MUST be immediately and manually added to `requirements.txt`. Failure to do so will result in container rebuild failures and is considered a critical development violation.
- **Node Modules Quarantine:** Frontend dependencies are handled via Docker anonymous volumes. Do not attempt to read or modify the host's `node_modules` directory.

## 8. The Experiment Ledger

No task, experiment, or significant bug fix is considered complete until it is logged in `EXPERIMENTS.md`.

- Every entry must contain: Date, Git Branch, Objective/Hypothesis, and Outcome/Result.
- AI Agents are instructed to halt workflows and refuse to close out tasks until this ledger is updated.



================================================
FILE: NSTAR_PHASES.md
================================================
# Project Roadmap: Decoupled Parallelism

This document outlines the macro-phases of the Resilient IoT Spatiotemporal Pipeline. We strictly adhere to a "Decoupled Parallelism" strategy: the frontend and backend are developed independently via a static data bridge until the mathematical architecture is completely stable.

## Phase 1: The Static Frontend Prototype & UX Validation

- **Focus:** Visualizing complex ML outputs efficiently in the browser.
- Generate a comprehensive, static `ui_payload.json` from the current "Iteration 0" PyTorch model.
- **Strict Constraint:** The payload MUST be generated exclusively from a quarantined **Visual Holdout Set** (e.g., the final 3 months of data) to prevent data leakage. Every payload must be stamped with 'Artifact Twinning' metadata (W&B Run ID, Git Hash, YAML config path) for traceability.
- Build the Next.js and Deck.gl frontend to ingest this static file.
- Validate the Zustand global clock, WebGL data filtering, and React rendering performance (60fps target).

_Status: In Progress._

## Phase 2: Backend Ablation Studies & Architecture Stabilization

- **Focus:** Freezing the ML architecture through rigorous scientific testing.
- Once the Phase 1 UI is rendering correctly, frontend development halts.
- Pivot back to the GPU server and utilize the Optuna/W&B framework to run ablation studies.
- **Strict Constraint:** All ablation studies must be executed purely by swapping centralized YAML configurations (`configs/train.yaml`).
  - _Study 1 (Topology):_ Disable the `DynamicGraphLearner`. Does the imputation MSE spike?
  - _Study 2 (Attention):_ Swap `GATv2Conv` for a standard GCN. Do we lose the temporal explainability?
  - _Study 3 (Target Expansion):_ Inject cross-modal features (e.g., weather) directly into the decoders.
- **Output:** A mathematically sound, final `best_pipeline.pt` with locked tensor shapes.

## Phase 3: Live Inference API & Spatial Integration

- **Focus:** Productionizing the frozen pipeline.
- Wrap the stable PyTorch pipeline in a highly concurrent FastAPI application.
- **Integrational Bottlenecks:** - Implement a Redis in-memory **Rolling Window Cache** to maintain the 12-timestep tensor requirements for instant inference.
  - Implement a strict **UUID Hashing Dictionary** to safely map shifting PyTorch integer indices to static physical sensor coordinates.
- Spin up Supabase (PostgreSQL + PostGIS) and ingest OpenStreetMap road networks.
- Connect the Next.js frontend to the live API, dropping the static `ui_payload.json` bridge.
- Implement PostGIS spatial interpolations to map point predictions onto continuous road segments.

# Resilient IoT Architecture Diagrams

These Mermaid diagrams represent the 4 architectural figures you can use as a baseline for your `draw.io` methodology figures.

## Figure 1: Data Pipeline
```mermaid
graph TD
    subgraph Data Sources
        SENSORS[traffic_sensors.json: Lat/Lon Coords]
        API[Google Places API New]
        C[Dynamic Time-Series Data: Sensor Readings]
        G[Target Variables: Raw Vehicle Counts]
    end

    subgraph StaticFeatureLoader
        SENSORS -- "lat, lon, radius (250m-500m)" --> API
        API -- "includedTypes (e.g. store, school)" --> STATS[Static Features: POI, Commercial Density, etc.]
        note[Note: Coords are NOT passed to model] -.-> STATS
    end

    subgraph Preprocessing
        B(StandardScaler Normalization)
        D(Artificial Masking)
        E(StandardScaler Normalization)
    end

    STATS --> B
    C --> D
    D --> E
    
    B --> F{PyTorch Dataset Loader}
    E --> F
    G --> F
    
    F --> H[Batch Dictionary]
    H --> I((ResilientIoTPipeline Model))
```

## Figure 2: Model Architecture
```mermaid
graph TD
    subgraph Inputs
        SF[Static Features: API Location Metadata]
        MTS[Masked Time-Series Data]
    end

    subgraph 1. Dynamic Graph Learner
        SF --> GAT[Multi-head GAT / Pairwise Similarity]
        GAT --> ADJ[Dynamic Adjacency Matrix]
        ADJ --> THRES[ReLU & Sparsity Threshold]
        THRES --> EI[edge_index & edge_weight]
    end

    subgraph 2. Masked Spatiotemporal Encoder
        MTS --> ST_ENC[Spatial: GATv2Conv Layers]
        EI --> ST_ENC
        ST_ENC --> FLAT[Flatten Spatial Output]
        FLAT --> T_ENC[Temporal: TransformerEncoder]
        T_ENC --> LS[Shared Latent Space Embedding]
    end

    subgraph 3. Decoupled Decoders
        LS --> IMP_DEC[Imputation Decoder: MLP]
        IMP_DEC --> REC[Reconstructed Masked Data]
        
        LS --> FOR_DEC[Forecasting Decoder: MLP]
        FOR_DEC --> ZINB[ZINB Parameters: μ, θ, π]
    end
    
    subgraph Loss Functions
        REC --> L1[MSE Imputation Loss]
        ZINB --> L2[ZINB NLL Loss]
        L1 -.-> JOINT_LOSS((Joint Training Objective))
        L2 -.-> JOINT_LOSS
    end
```

## Figure 3: MLOps Architecture
```mermaid
graph TD
    subgraph Centralized Configuration
        TRAIN_YML[configs/train.yaml]
        SWEEP_YML[configs/sweep.yaml]
    end

    subgraph Dockerized Execution Environments
        AGENT[Agent Workspace: Development]
        CPU[Mac Host: CPU Debugging]
        GPU[Remote Server: GPU Training / Sweeps]
        
        AGENT -.-> CPU
        AGENT -.-> GPU
    end

    TRAIN_YML --> TRAIN_PY[src/train.py: Orchestrator]
    SWEEP_YML --> TUNE_PY[src/tune.py: Optuna Sweeps]
    TUNE_PY --> TRAIN_PY

    TRAIN_PY --> WANDB[(Weights & Biases)]
    WANDB --> OPTUNA[(Optuna SQLite DB)]

    TRAIN_PY --> EXP_PY[export_ui_payload.py]
    EXP_PY --> UI_PAYLOAD[ui_payload.json with Traceability Metadata]
    EXP_PY -.-> VISUAL_HOLDOUT[Strict Visual Holdout Set]
```

## Figure 4: Frontend & Database Architecture
```mermaid
graph TD
    subgraph Browser Client
        NEXTJS[Next.js + React]
        ZUSTAND[Zustand State Management: useTimeStore]
        DECKGL[Deck.gl over MapLibre GL JS]
        
        NEXTJS --> ZUSTAND
        ZUSTAND --> DECKGL
        
        subgraph Deck.gl Visual Layers
            NODE[ScatterplotLayer: Predictions / Uncertainty]
            EDGE[ArcLayer/LineLayer: Attention Topology]
            HEAT[PathLayer: PostGIS Road Heatmaps]
        end
        DECKGL --> NODE
        DECKGL --> EDGE
        DECKGL --> HEAT
    end

    subgraph Backend Infrastructure
        UI_PAYLOAD[Phase 1: Static ui_payload.json]
        
        subgraph Phase 2: Live Integration
            FASTAPI[FastAPI Model Wrapper]
            CACHE[(Redis: 12-Timestep Rolling Cache)]
            DB[(Supabase: PostgreSQL + PostGIS)]
            
            FASTAPI --> CACHE
            FASTAPI --> DB
        end
    end

    UI_PAYLOAD --> NEXTJS
    FASTAPI -.-> NEXTJS
```

FILE: docs/gpu_usage_report.md

# GPU Usage, Multi-GPU Training, and Device Specification Report

This report provides an in-depth context-gathering analysis of how GPU resources are utilized, configured, and managed within the ML workspace. It is prepared from the perspective of the **Interrogator** to assist development, systems architecture, and deployment alignment.

---

## 1. Executive Summary

- **GPU Utilization:** Training runs (`src/train.py`) and Optuna hyperparameter sweeps (`src/tune.py`) dynamically offload workloads to a CUDA-compatible GPU if PyTorch detects one. All deep learning components (encoders, decoders, loss calculations) and model tensors are dynamically pushed to the active device. Conversely, the UI payload generation (`src/export_ui_payload.py`) is explicitly hardcoded to execute on the CPU.
- **Multi-GPU Training:** **Not currently implemented at the PyTorch model-level.** Single training runs execute on a single GPU using standard sequential processing. However, the container orchestration environment supports infrastructure-level parallel execution by exposing multiple GPUs to independent containers/runs.
- **GPU Specification:** The GPU environment is controlled using Docker Compose GPU device reservations (`device_ids: ["0", "1"]`). At the code level, PyTorch defaults to using the first visible device (`cuda:0` / standard `"cuda"` device index), with no code-level mechanism currently exposing selection controls or supporting PyTorch distributed training libraries.

---

## 2. Deep-Dive: How the GPU is Used

GPU integration in our MLOps system utilizes a hybrid approach: containerized environments for isolation and dynamic Torch device placement for execution.

### A. The Container Execution Layer
Training and sweeps on the remote server run inside a headless, optimized CUDA-enabled Docker container:
- **Base Image:** `pytorch/pytorch:2.1.0-cuda12.1-cudnn8-runtime` (defined in `Dockerfile.gpu`).
- **Accessing GPUs:** Built using the local GPU server configuration via `docker compose build gpu-runner` and run using:
  ```bash
  docker compose run --rm gpu-runner python src/train.py --epochs 50 --batch_size 128
  ```

### B. PyTorch Device Selection Logic
Within `src/train.py`, the active device is resolved dynamically at runtime:
```python
# Device configuration
device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")
logger.info(f"Using device: {device}")
```
Once the device is identified:
1. **Model Placement:** The entire `ResilientIoTPipeline` architecture is pushed to the target device:
   ```python
   model = ResilientIoTPipeline(...).to(device)
   ```
2. **Tensor/Batch Transfer:** During both the training and validation epochs, loaded tensor batches are moved to the GPU dynamically inside the data loops:
   ```python
   batch = {k: v.to(device) if torch.is_tensor(v) else v for k, v in batch.items()}
   ```

### C. Sweeps & Tuning
The Optuna sweep framework in `src/tune.py` executes trial objectives by calling `run_training(config=config, init_wandb=False)`. Consequently, all hyperparameter search trials run inside the `gpu-runner` context and utilize the active GPU device dynamically.

### D. CPU-Only Exceptions
The UI payload generator (`src/export_ui_payload.py`) explicitly targets the CPU for post-processing and export steps:
```python
device = torch.device("cpu")  # CPU runner as specified
logger.info(f"Device: {device}")
```
This is a deliberate architectural decision to ensure payload compilation does not require GPU resources or compete with training containers.

---

## 3. Multi-GPU Implementation Status

### A. Single-Model Multi-GPU: **Not Implemented**
The core training script (`src/train.py`) utilizes standard single-device execution. The code **lacks** any implementations of:
- `torch.nn.DataParallel` (DP)
- `torch.nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel` (DDP)
- Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP)
- High-level orchestration integrations (e.g., PyTorch Ignite, PyTorch Lightning, Hugging Face Accelerate).

As a result, a single model training process cannot split its batch size or model layers across multiple physical cards simultaneously.

### B. Infrastructure-Level Parallelism: **Supported**
Although single-model training is single-GPU, the environment supports running **multiple independent experiments concurrently** on different GPUs on the server. 

According to `NSTAR_MLOPS.md` (§1, Parallel Execution Rule):
> *"Because the GPU runner mounts the local directory (`.:/workspace`), you cannot run multiple branches from the same physical directory. To run experiments in parallel, clone the repository into separate directories on the server and execute `docker compose` independently in each."*

By isolating physical directories and running independent containers, multiple hyperparameter trials or architecture explorations can run simultaneously on different GPU indexes.

---

## 4. How the GPU to Use is Specified

Currently, the selection of which physical GPU to use is managed outside of the Python code itself, moving from Docker Compose to the operating system level.

### A. Docker Compose Resource Allocation
In `docker-compose.yml`, the `gpu-runner` service specifies which hardware GPUs are mapped into the container environment:

```yaml
  # Run on GPU server for training.
  gpu-runner:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile.gpu
    container_name: gpu-executor
    volumes:
      - .:/workspace
    environment:
      - WANDB_API_KEY=${WANDB_API_KEY}
    # This block maps the physical server GPUs into the container
    deploy:
      resources:
        reservations:
          devices:
            - driver: nvidia
              device_ids: ["0", "1"]
              capabilities: [gpu]
```

This configuration reserves physical GPU cards with index `0` and `1` and exposes them to the container under `/dev/nvidia0` and `/dev/nvidia1`.

### B. Python/PyTorch Default Behavior
Inside the container, PyTorch sees both GPUs as `cuda:0` and `cuda:1`.
However, because `train.py` queries `torch.device("cuda")` without a specific index, PyTorch will automatically default to selecting **device 0** (`cuda:0`). 

### C. Targeting a Specific GPU
There is currently:
- **No** CLI flag in `train.py` or `tune.py` (e.g., `--device cuda:1`).
- **No** parameter in `configs/train.yaml` specifying target device indices.

To target a specific GPU (for example, to force a run onto the second card, `1`), you must specify the standard NVIDIA runtime environment variable `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` when launching the container command. For example:

```bash
docker compose run --rm -e CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1 gpu-runner python src/train.py
```
This forces the container to only expose physical GPU `1` to the training script, which PyTorch will then bind to as its default `cuda:0` device.

---

## 5. Architectural Recommendations (Next Steps)

For the next phases of development (as described in `NSTAR_PHASES.md`), the following enhancements are recommended to improve GPU efficiency and robustness:

1. **Add Code-Level Device Configuration:**
   Introduce a `device` key in `configs/train.yaml` (e.g., `device: "cuda:0"` or `device: "cuda:1"`) and support a `--device` CLI override in `src/train.py`. This will eliminate reliance on environment variables for targeting specific cards.
   
2. **Implement PyTorch DDP for Scaled Training:**
   If dataset size or sequences scale in Phase 2/3, implement `DistributedDataParallel` in `src/train.py`. DDP is highly efficient and standard for multi-GPU training.
   
3. **Add GPU Telemetry Tracking in W&B:**
   Add system telemetry metrics (GPU temperature, memory usage, and processor load) in W&B dashboard configurations. This helps identify memory bottlenecks (e.g., when scaling the dynamic feedforward dimension during Optuna sweeps).

# Agentic Workflow Summary

## Phase 1: Infrastructure & Security

The foundation of this workflow is an isolated, disposable environment where your code is safe, but the agent's memory is intentionally volatile.

- The Unified Dockerfile: Your image is built on a lightweight Python base. It installs system dependencies, Node.js (for Gemini CLI), and native curl binaries (for Claude Code), alongside your Python ML requirements.

- Volume Mounts (The Portal): You map your local Mac directory to /workspace inside the container. This ensures your code, data, and models are permanently saved on your host machine, while the agent edits them live from inside the container.

- Security (.env & .gitignore): API keys are never baked into the Docker image or stored globally in ~/.zshrc. They are strictly kept in a local .env file, which is added to .gitignore to prevent accidental GitHub leaks.

- The --rm Mandate: You boot the environment using docker compose run --rm agent-workspace bash. The --rm flag is critical: it ensures that the moment you exit the shell, the container and the agent's internal chat history are completely destroyed, giving you a pristine state for your next session.

## Phase 2: The Agent's Operating System (System Prompt)

The agent is governed by a strict system prompt (saved as GEMINI.md or CLAUDE.md in your workspace) that establishes its rules of engagement.

- Plan First: The agent is forbidden from writing complex execution code immediately. It must first write a step-by-step architectural plan to plan.md and wait for human approval.

- Safety First (Git): The agent must execute git commit to create local rollback points before refactoring working code or beginning new major tasks.

- The Experiment Ledger: The agent is mandated to maintain an EXPERIMENTS.md file, logging the date, active branch, prompt used, and the mathematical outcome of every model training run.

## Phase 3: The "Offensive ML" Coding Paradigm

Machine learning prototyping requires "Offensive Programming." The agent is instructed to write code that fails fast and hard rather than silently coercing bad data.

- Ruthless Assertions: The agent must explicitly assert tensor shapes, dataframe columns, and exact data types at the top of every major function.

- Let it Burn: Generic except: blocks are banned. If a matrix dimension mismatches, the script must crash immediately and print the full traceback for diagnosis.

- NaN Tripwires: The agent must place explicit checks after transformations to crash the script if unexpected NaNs or infinite values are introduced.

- No Silent Drops: Functions like .dropna() are banned unless explicitly approved in the data cleaning plan.

## Phase 4: The Execution Loop (Human-in-the-Loop Orchestration)

You act as the Senior Engineer; the AI acts as the Junior Developer. You do not ask the agent to "build a pipeline." You micro-batch its tasks.

- The Blind EDA: You begin by asking the agent to analyze the raw data without telling it what the data is or what your goal is. This forces the agent to read the literal matrix (identifying true anomalies like time-series gaps) rather than hallucinating a perfect dataset.

- The Common Preprocessing Pipeline: Based on the EDA, the agent builds a single, hardened data pipeline (handling resampling, imputation, and spatial joins) that outputs clean matrices (e.g., .npy files). This is committed to the main Git branch.

- The Crash-and-Fix Loop: When the agent writes a script, you run it. When it crashes (due to your offensive programming assertions), you do not fix it yourself. You paste the exact traceback back to the agent and instruct it to resolve the anomaly.

## Phase 5: State Management (Git Branching & Memory Wipes)

To prevent the agent's context window from degrading into a polluted mess of broken PyTorch tensors and overlapping logic, you strictly sandbox your experiments.

- Model Sandboxing: Every new model architecture (e.g., XGBoost, Random Forest, Neural Networks) gets its own Git branch branched off main.

- The Context Wipe: Before starting a new branch or moving to a radically new step in the pipeline, you explicitly clear the agent's memory (using /clear or restarting the CLI).

- Re-Grounding: Upon starting a fresh branch or cleared session, you point the agent to the current working state of the files and the EXPERIMENTS.md ledger, ensuring it builds exclusively on clean, working logic.

FILE: docs/tuning.md

# Tuning Runbook

This document explains how to use the Optuna hyperparameter tuning framework for the ResilientIoT model. The tuning process has been upgraded to support multiple independent experiments through persistent SQLite namespacing.

## 1. Quick Start (Interactive Setup)

The easiest way to start a tuning sweep is to use the interactive CLI. Simply run:

```bash
python src/tune.py --use_mock_data
```

*(Omit `--use_mock_data` to run on the real parquet datasets).*

If you do not provide `--storage` and `--study-name`, the script will automatically discover existing SQLite databases in your current directory and prompt you to select one (or create a new one). It will then list the studies within that database and let you select an existing study to resume or create a new one.

## 2. Command-Line Usage (Automated Scripts)

For automated scripts or remote servers, you can pass the namespacing arguments directly to bypass the interactive prompts:

```bash
python src/tune.py \
    --storage sqlite:///my_experiment.db \
    --study-name "dropout-layer-test" \
    --n-trials 10
```

### CLI Arguments

*   `--storage`: The SQLite connection string (e.g., `sqlite:///optuna.db`). If omitted, you will be prompted.
*   `--study-name`: The name of the specific experiment. If omitted, you will be prompted.
*   `--config`: Path to the base YAML config (default: `configs/train.yaml`).
*   `--n-trials`: Number of Optuna trials to run (default: 2).
*   `--use_mock_data`: Use synthetic mock data instead of real data (useful for local CPU testing).

## 3. Configuration Strategy

We maintain a strict separation between base configurations and search spaces:

*   **`configs/train.yaml`**: This file contains the **fixed, base values** for every parameter. If you add a new parameter to the model, you must define its default value here.
*   **`configs/sweep.yaml`**: This file defines **what is being tuned**. It uses **dot-notation** (e.g., `model.dropout`) to map the Optuna suggestion directly into the nested dictionary loaded from `train.yaml`. 

To sweep a new parameter:
1. Ensure it exists in `train.yaml` (e.g., `model: { new_param: 0.5 }`).
2. Add it to `sweep.yaml` using dot notation:
   ```yaml
   parameters:
     model.new_param:
       distribution: uniform
       min: 0.1
       max: 0.9
   ```

## 4. Metadata Stamping and Traceability

Every trial executed by `src/tune.py` is automatically stamped with the current Git commit hash and branch name. 

You can view these stamps in two ways:

1.  **Optuna Dashboard**:
    ```bash
    pip install optuna-dashboard
    optuna-dashboard sqlite:///optuna.db
    ```
    Open the web UI and click on a study. The Git metadata will be visible in the "User Attributes" column of the trial table.

2.  **Analysis Script**: The analysis script (see below) extracts and analyzes all completed trials from the specified study.

## 5. High-Resolution Analysis

To analyze the hyperparameter importance and correlations for a specific study, use the `src/analysis.py` script. You must point it to the correct SQLite database and study name:

```bash
# List all studies in the database
python src/analysis.py --storage sqlite:///optuna.db list

# Perform detailed analysis of a specific study
python src/analysis.py --storage sqlite:///optuna.db analyze my-experiment

# Promote best parameters to a config file
python src/analysis.py --storage sqlite:///optuna.db promote my-experiment --config configs/train/train.yaml
```

### Key Features of Analysis

*   **Artifact Twinning Verification**: The script verifies that each trial is linked to a specific Git commit and branch.
*   **Hyperparameter Importance**: Uses Optuna's built-in importance algorithms to identify which parameters most influence the validation loss.
*   **Parameter Promotion**: Automatically updates your base `train.yaml` with the winning hyperparameters, ensuring a seamless transition from tuning to production training.

FILE: frontend/src/app/GlobalErrorReporter.tsx
================================================
"use client";
import { useEffect } from "react";

export function GlobalErrorReporter() {
  useEffect(() => {
    const post = (payload: Record<string, unknown>) =>
      fetch("/api/log", {
        method: "POST",
        headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
        body: JSON.stringify(payload),
      }).catch(() => {});

    const onError = (event: ErrorEvent) => {
      post({
        type: "onerror",
        message: event.message,
        source: event.filename,
        lineno: event.lineno,
        colno: event.colno,
        stack: event.error?.stack ?? null,
      });
    };

    const onUnhandled = (event: PromiseRejectionEvent) => {
      post({
        type: "unhandledrejection",
        message: String(event.reason),
        stack: (event.reason as { stack?: string } | null)?.stack ?? null,
      });
    };

    window.addEventListener("error", onError);
    window.addEventListener("unhandledrejection", onUnhandled);
    return () => {
      window.removeEventListener("error", onError);
      window.removeEventListener("unhandledrejection", onUnhandled);
    };
  }, []);
  return null;
}
